+# The shape of the book, not its size: what confidential transfers on the XRP Ledger hide
+
+*Status, before anything else: this is a research project on the XRP Ledger's
+Devnet test network. It is not audited, not production-ready, and not safe to put
+value behind. Its signer refuses every network but Devnet.*
+
+Can a token move confidentially on the XRP Ledger? Yes, under a standard called
+XLS-96, and today only on Devnet. Read on 2026-10-22 at 03:25 UTC, its amendment,
+`ConfidentialTransfer`, was enabled on Devnet, and supported but not enabled on
+Mainnet and Testnet. That reading goes stale. The repository has a script that
+takes a fresh one.
+
+What XLS-96 hides is narrower than the word *confidential* suggests. It hides
+balances, and the amounts of transfers between confidential balances. It does not
+hide who pays whom, when, or how often. It does not hide amounts entering or
+leaving confidential form. And it hides nothing from the token's issuer.
+
+Since mid-August I have been building an application on it to find out exactly
+where that line falls, by running transactions rather than by reading the
+specification. This post is what I found, what the reviews of my own work caught,
+and what went back upstream. The project is called W-CONF. The code and its claims
+register are public at
+[github.com/nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions](https://github.com/nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions),
+and a read-only demo is at
+[xrpl-confidential-positions.vercel.app](https://xrpl-confidential-positions.vercel.app).
+
+Ids such as *E-58* in this post are rows in the project's claims register,
+[`CLAIMS.md`](https://github.com/nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions/blob/main/CLAIMS.md).
+Each row says what supports it. Where this post and a row disagree, the row wins.
+
+I built it with Claude Code as the coding agent. The reviews described below were
+run by other models (Codex and Gemini) and by separate Claude sessions, each given
+a different packet.
+
+* * *
+
+## What XLS-96 is, briefly
+
+The XRP Ledger is public: anyone can read every account and every transaction.
+Multi-Purpose Tokens (MPTs, XLS-33) are one of its token types. XLS-96,
+Confidential Transfers for MPTs, lets a holder keep an MPT balance encrypted on
+that public ledger.
+
+Each confidential balance is an ElGamal ciphertext. A holder registers their own
+encryption key when they opt in. The ledger still has to stop anyone spending more
+than they hold, so every transaction that spends from a confidential balance
+carries zero-knowledge proofs, which validators check without learning the
+amount.
+
+A position moves through four transactions:
+
+- **Convert** moves tokens from a public balance into confidential form. The
+  amount is published.
+- **Send** moves an amount from one confidential balance to another. The amount is
+  hidden. The sender and receiver are not.
+- **MergeInbox** moves what a holder has received into the balance they can spend.
+- **ConvertBack** moves tokens back to a public balance. The amount is published.
+
+Only Send hides an amount.
+
+The specification, XLS-0096, is the work of Murat Cenk, Aanchal Malhotra, Ayo
+Akinyele, Peter Chen, Shawn Xie and Yinyi Qian, and has been `Final` since
+2026-09-03 (E-1). The compact sigma protocol behind its send proof is the work of
+Tess Dore and Murat Cenk at RippleX Research. The reference cryptography is
+[`XRPLF/mpt-crypto`](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto). This project builds on
+their work and claims none of it.
+
+* * *
+
+## What it hides, and what it does not
+
+**Hidden from the public:** each holder's balance, and the amount of each Send.
+
+**Public, always:**
+
+- The sender and receiver of every transfer, and when it happened, so how often
+  anyone transacts (P-4, P-11).
+- Every amount converted in or out, each one separately.
+- The token's total supply, and the total held in confidential form.
+- That a balance is exactly zero, in three cases, below.
+
+**Who can read every balance:** the issuer, always. XLS-96 keeps a second copy of
+each confidential balance, encrypted to the issuer's key, and no setting turns it
+off (P-9). An auditor can read them too, if the token names one, and only one is
+supported (E-11). I ran this rather than inferring it. The holder, the issuer and
+the auditor each decrypted the same balance, 500, and each read 475 after a
+25-unit Send (E-58).
+
+**Who can spend:** only whoever holds the holder's encryption secret, because a
+Send's proof cannot be made without it (E-33). Where that secret is derived from
+the account's master seed, as this project derives it, whoever holds the seed
+holds the secret too. The issuer cannot spend a holder's balance. If the token
+allows clawback, the issuer can destroy it, crediting no one, and this project's
+tokens allow clawback (E-12). Unless the token's flags were made unchangeable when
+it was created, the issuer can also turn on locking later and freeze balances.
+That second power I read in rippled's source and did not run (E-290).
+
+**Zero is sometimes visible.** XLS-96 writes a fixed encryption of zero, which
+anyone can recompute from public inputs, in three places: a new confidential
+balance, an inbox that a MergeInbox has just emptied, and a position clawed back.
+So enrolment is publicly visible. The English documentation said this value could
+not be told apart from other ciphertexts. I recomputed it from public inputs and
+matched it byte for byte against live Devnet state (F-1, E-59), reported it, and
+the English page was corrected in September. A balance spent down to zero holds a
+randomised encryption of zero, and is not recognisable (E-211).
+
+**The public numbers bound the hidden ones.** If a holder converts in 500, receives
+nothing, and converts back 400, anyone can work out that the other 100 is either
+still held or has left by confidential Send. No key is needed. I first wrote that
+the 100 *"left by transfer"*. That overstated what an observer learns, and it is
+recorded as a correction (R-7).
+
+* * *
+
+## No rotation, no recovery
+
+A registered key can never be changed (E-9). XLS-96 has no key rotation and no key
+recovery (P-10). If a holder loses their key, and any seed it could be re-derived
+from, the balance cannot be spent. The only remedy is an issuer clawback, which
+destroys the balance.
+
+A draft standard, XLS-99, by Aanchal Malhotra and Yinyi Qian, proposes rotation for
+holder, issuer and auditor keys, and recovery from key loss. On 2026-10-22,
+rippled's development branch and its release candidate `3.5.0-rc1` declared its
+amendment as not supported, and Mainnet, Testnet and Devnet did not know it (E-353).
+So there is no rotation or recovery in XLS-96, or on Mainnet, Testnet or Devnet,
+today.
+
+* * *
+
+## The example: a confidential receivables facility
+
+To test the standard against something with stakes, I built the core of a
+receivables facility. A bank pays a large buyer's suppliers early, against their
+invoices. How much a supplier draws says a lot about its cash position, so these
+amounts are commercially sensitive.
+
+The bank issues a token. A Dedicated Account, an ordinary XRPL account, holds the
+float and converts it into confidential form once. Suppliers enrol, draw down by
+confidential Send, and repay the same way. At the end, what is left of the float is
+retired in tranches by ConvertBack. It ran on Devnet on 2026-08-25, in 28
+validated facility transactions besides setting up the token (E-146).
+
+**Anyone can see** the size of the float, because converting it publishes the
+amount. They can see which suppliers enrolled, who drew and repaid, on which days,
+and how often. And they can see the size of every retirement tranche, because
+each ConvertBack publishes its own amount (F-24). My own walkthrough first said a
+ConvertBack published only *"the aggregate change"*. The validated transactions
+showed each tranche in plaintext.
+
+**Hidden from the public:** each supplier's limit, the size of each drawdown and
+repayment, and what each supplier still owes. The bank, as issuer, sees all of it.
+
+So it hides the shape of the book, not its size. An early version of the design
+claimed the facility *"shows nothing about which supplier drew how much"* (R-1).
+Which supplier drew, and when, is public.
+
+Two limits of that run. First, it read the ledger through a public Devnet node.
+The design requires a node on the same machine, and a local Devnet node never
+finished syncing on the hardware I had, so I accepted that as a named risk (E-148,
+E-151). Second, one test driver played the bank and every supplier and held every
+seed. The run shows that these transactions are well formed and that rippled
+accepts them. It does not show how custody would work.
+
+* * *
+
+## Reading the specification, and running it
+
+Several of the things above I learned only by running them:
+
+- That three different keys decrypt the same balance, through a change (E-58).
+- That the encryption of zero matches a value anyone can recompute (F-1).
+- That a balance spent to zero is not recognisable. My own observer page had
+  labelled every ciphertext that was not the fixed zero as *"Non-zero"*, and a
+  holder whose balance decrypted to 0 disproved it (E-211).
+- That a failing transaction is not simply rejected. A `tec` result is applied to
+  the ledger. The submission parameter `fail_hard` asks the node to discard it
+  instead. I ran both on Devnet with a plain Payment that fails with `tecNO_AUTH`:
+  the same signed bytes were applied without the flag, and discarded with it
+  (E-149).
+
+That last one matters more for confidential transactions than for plain ones,
+because a `tec` keeps the transaction's fields, ciphertexts included. If a signer
+reads a wrong destination key from a dishonest node, the amount is published
+encrypted to that key for good, even though the transaction fails. That case I
+read in rippled's source and did not run (F-23).
+
+Running is not a substitute for reading what others have already found. Several
+things I first recorded as findings turned out to be issues that Trail of Bits'
+April 2026 review had already found and Ripple had fixed.
+
+* * *
+
+## What the reviews caught
+
+Design decisions and fixes went through rounds of adversarial review. Reviewers
+were given deliberately different packets, so that their agreement would mean
+more than one reading copied three times. The repository keeps 61 numbered review
+records, including the ones that found me wrong. A few of the catches:
+
+- **A secret not wiped from memory, behind a comment explaining why it could not
+  be.** The comment said the secret's type belonged to an outside library. It was
+  the project's own type, and one search of its file showed it had no wiping at
+  all (E-200).
+- **A design claim that four reviewers independently ruled unsound.** I had
+  written that a read-only web tier *"moves no trust boundary"*. It added no
+  signing authority. But it re-served, to any local user, data the project
+  protects with file permissions (E-230).
+- **A release gate that passed a Content Security Policy every browser refused.**
+  The gate hashed inline scripts over the file's bytes. Browsers hash the text they
+  parse, which differs. Only loading the pages in a real browser showed it (E-334).
+- **Fixes that did not fix.** In one review's second round, five of the first
+  round's fixes had not discharged their findings. Only the reviewer that ran each
+  finding's own example against the fixed code found them (E-345).
+
+The lesson I keep relearning: fixing something and describing the fix are two
+acts that fail separately. A reviewer who reads my summary of a finding will tick
+the summary.
+
+* * *
+
+## Checks that catch a false sentence
+
+The working rule is that a claim needs its evidence before it is written. The
+claims register holds each established claim with what supports it, and twelve
+standing prohibitions, P-1 to P-12, on what may not be claimed at all: that XLS-96
+hides identities, that holders are private from the issuer, that it supports key
+rotation, any benchmark without its hardware and method, and any claim that a
+source does *not* say something without the search that would show otherwise.
+
+Prose drifts anyway, so some of it is enforced by checks that run in CI. One fails
+when a retracted phrase reappears without its correction. One fails when an
+observation filed or graded for upstream says a source does not say something,
+without the search recorded beside it. One keeps the register's derived indexes in
+step with it.
+
+They do not catch everything. The register keeps eight corrections, R-1 to R-8,
+each a sentence this project published and later found false. The last one, R-8,
+said every confidential transaction needs both of a holder's keys; a MergeInbox
+needs only the account key. It was on the public demo until a reviewer found it
+repeated in a diagram caption. And on 2026-10-18 the register still listed as open four
+filings that upstream had fixed in September (E-316). Nothing re-checked them until
+I surveyed every filing at source. That survey is now a gate on anything I publish
+about them, including this post.
+
+* * *
+
+## What went upstream
+
+Since 2026-08-26 this project has filed eleven issues in four XRPLF repositories.
+Their state on 2026-10-22, read at source (E-353):
+
+- **Four resolved in September.** Three issues against the XLS-0096 text were
+  resolved in the merged standard. The documentation's sentence calling the zero
+  value indistinguishable was corrected on the English page. The Japanese
+  translation was made from the text before the fix, and still carries it.
+- **The mpt-crypto build.** Its lockfile pinned an OpenSSL revision that the
+  documented package remote did not serve. Fixed on mpt-crypto's main branch on
+  2026-10-21, not yet in a release. I re-ran the check against the merged commit,
+  and it resolves.
+- **The fixed zero, from JavaScript.** mpt-crypto's WASM and npm packages did not
+  expose the function that computes the fixed encryption of zero, which is the
+  check an outside observer most needs. A maintainer agreed and a pull request is
+  approved and not merged. I have offered to write the JavaScript wrapper once it
+  is released.
+- **Three filed on 2026-10-18, against mpt-crypto, with no reply yet:** an
+  ambiguity in what the compact sigma paper's nonce input covers; a WASM build
+  script that fails outside its pinned toolchain; and a proof-size total in the
+  paper that assumes a ciphertext component is sent once, where rippled and
+  XLS-0096 send it per recipient.
+- **A question to XRPL-Standards**, whether a confidential balance is meant to stay
+  invisible to vault accounting, has had no reply in 57 days.
+- **A filing to rippled that listed only things done well** was closed without
+  comment the day it was filed. It fit neither of that repository's issue
+  templates. The project's filing rules now forbid a positives-only filing to a bug
+  tracker.
+
+I do not call any of these new, or unreported. Three reviews of this code exist.
+Trail of Bits' April 2026 report is the one I could read and check my findings
+against; a later revision of it could not be obtained. The Sherlock XRP Ledger
+Attackathon was a contest that covered Confidential Transfers, and its findings are
+not public. A Zellic scan of mpt-crypto is known to me only through three
+identifiers an upstream issue cites. Of the three, only Trail of Bits' April
+report could be checked.
+
+* * *
+
+## What it does not show
+
+- **No run against a local node.** Every Devnet read went to the public cluster
+  (E-151).
+- **Custody is accepted, not solved.** The issuer can erase or freeze the
+  facility's float. The Dedicated Account's key both moves the float and decrypts
+  every amount sent to or from it, so those two powers cannot be held apart
+  (E-266).
+- **The key derivation is unpublished.** It is specified in this repository only,
+  so a holder with their master seed but not this repository cannot reconstruct
+  their key. Treat it as lock-in until it is published.
+- **A hostile process running as the same user** can read the encryption secret
+  out of memory.
+
+* * *
+
+## Try it
+
+The demo is static pages built from one snapshot of Devnet. It signs nothing and
+holds no keys. It shows what an observer reads of four test accounts, including
+which balances are visibly zero; which encrypted copies the issuer can read,
+without publishing the figures; and what each kind of key can do. It does not show the facility, whose record is in the repository.
+
+To transact, you run the signer yourself, on your own machine. The README explains
+how to build it and run the local version of the site. It does not yet walk
+through creating a holder profile and funded Devnet accounts, which a transaction
+needs.
+
+The repository has the Rust signer, the TypeScript facility, the read-only web
+viewer, the claims register and the review records, under Apache-2.0.
+
+If something in this is wrong, please tell me, by an issue on the repository. The
+register has a section for corrections, and it has eight entries.
+
+* * *
+

+# LinkedIn post — draft
+
+Plain text below the rule; LinkedIn renders no Markdown. `<BLOG-URL>` is a
+placeholder until the blog post has its address.
+
+---
+
+Is confidential transfer possible on the XRP Ledger? Yes, on Devnet only: read on 2026-10-22, the ConfidentialTransfer amendment (XLS-96) was enabled on Devnet, and not on Mainnet or Testnet.
+
+It hides less than the word suggests. I built the core of a receivables facility on it and ran it on Devnet to find out exactly what an outside observer sees.
+
+Hidden from the public: each holder's balance, and the amount of each transfer between confidential balances.
+
+Public: who pays whom, when, and how often. Every amount converted into or out of confidential form. Some zero balances, which anyone can recognise.
+
+The issuer can read every balance, always. No setting turns that off.
+
+There is no key rotation or recovery in XLS-96, or on Mainnet, Testnet or Devnet today. A draft standard, XLS-99, proposes both.
+
+In the facility, that means the size of the float, which suppliers drew on which days, and every retirement tranche are public. Each supplier's limit, the size of each drawdown and what each supplier owes are not. It hides the shape of the book, not its size.
+
+Along the way, eleven issues went upstream to XRPLF repositories. Four were resolved in September, and a fifth was fixed on 2026-10-21, not yet in a release.
+
+This is a research project on a test network. It is not audited, and not production-ready.
+
+The write-up: <BLOG-URL>
+A read-only demo: https://xrpl-confidential-positions.vercel.app
+The code and the claims register: https://github.com/nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions
+
+XLS-96 is the work of Murat Cenk, Aanchal Malhotra, Ayo Akinyele, Peter Chen, Shawn Xie and Yinyi Qian, with Tess Dore and Murat Cenk's compact sigma protocol.

# CLAIMS register

⚠ **`tapFailHard` in this document is THIS PROJECT'S SHORTHAND and appears zero times in rippled** — the real names are `fail_hard` (the RPC parameter) and `TapFailHard` (the internal apply flag). See [`docs/VOCABULARY.md`](docs/VOCABULARY.md). The code always used the real one.

**The register.** Every capability claim in a README, demo, post or conversation gets an entry here with the test result or primary citation that supports it. **Before publishing anything, diff the new claims against this register.**

**Human-gated checklist, not automation.** CI cannot judge whether prose overclaims. Do not add a CI job and call it solved.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Navigate** | [`docs/CLAIMS-INDEX.md`](docs/CLAIMS-INDEX.md) — one line per row, generated. ⚠ Do not hand-edit it; `make check-claims-index` fails if it drifts. |
| **Statuses** | `SUPPORTED` evidence attached · `PENDING` asserted, evidence not attached, **may not be published** · `BLOCKED` never claim · `RETRACTED` was claimed, found wrong — **keep the row** |
| **Sections** | [Standing prohibitions](#standing-prohibitions--never-claim) · [Established](#established) · [Pending](#pending--asserted-not-yet-supportable) · [Findings](#findings--verified-file-upstream-before-publishing) · [Retracted](#retracted) |
| **History** | [Revision history](#revision-history) — moved to the end 2026-09-04. ⚠ It was **5,358 characters of unbroken prose above the first heading**, so nothing in this file was visible without scrolling past it. |

---

## Standing prohibitions — never claim

| # | Claim | Why |
|---|---|---|
| P-1 | That this work makes XRPL quantum-safe or quantum-resistant | It does not |
| P-2 | That an application-layer PQ scheme protects funds at the ledger level | An adversary who breaks secp256k1 signs a valid classical transaction and the network accepts it |
| P-3 | That any amendment is live or enabled, without a dated `feature` RPC check | Amendment state is a reading, not knowledge |
| P-4 | That a Confidential MPT demo hides identities | It hides amounts and balances. Sender and receiver remain public |
| P-5 | That anything here is trustless, non-custodial, decentralised, audited, production-ready or secure | None of it is; ADR-0001 names the trusted parties |
| P-6 | That anything is compliant with regulation in a named jurisdiction | Not a compliance project |
| P-7 | That analysis of published work is original research | Cite Cenk, Malhotra, Akinyele, Chen, Xie, Qian by name where the work is theirs. **Add Tess Dore (2026-08-16):** the compact sigma protocol, the twisted-ElGamal analysis and the Bulletproofs++ analysis are Dore & Cenk, RippleX Research — Dore is *not* an XLS-0096 author and is easy to omit by copying E-1's list |
| P-8 | Any benchmark number without hardware, parameter set and methodology | Includes re-quoting XLS-0096 §14.2's "laptop-class CPU" timings — see B-1 |
| P-9 | That holders are private from the issuer | The issuer's decryption mirror is mandatory. No configuration makes this false |
| P-10 | That XLS-96 supports key rotation or key recovery | It supports neither, at any level |
| P-11 | That confidential transfers hide *who* transacts, or *when*, or *how often* | Only amounts are hidden. The counterparty graph and event timing are fully public |
| P-12 | **Any claim that a source does NOT say something — *nothing documents it*, *no such field*, *not exported*, *nobody has called it out*, *I could not find it* — without the search that would falsify it recorded alongside** | It is an assertion about a whole corpus and one search settles it. **Three graded rows failed this way on 2026-08-26** (E-155, E-156), two of them **positives**: U-15 quoted a sentence XLS-0096 has never contained; U-12 said a property was *"not called out anywhere I found"* when the spec states it **twice**, once in wording identical to the benefit claimed as an original reading. ⚠ **This shape survives review because it does not look like a claim** — a quotation has quotation marks, a benchmark visibly wants a methodology, and *"nobody has documented this"* reads as modesty. Gated by `tools/check-negative-claims.ts` |

## Established

| # | Claim | Evidence | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| E-1 | XLS-0096 authors: Cenk, Malhotra, Akinyele, Chen, Xie, Qian; status Draft, created 2026-01-15. ⚠ **Superseded upstream on 2026-09-03.** XLS-0096 PR #604, *"Update and finalize XLS-96"*, merged as `33ea4ca66` and set `status: Final`. It reads `Final` at `58ccd3047` and at `master` `47fa15357`, read on 2026-10-18 (E-322) | Spec header, at the time; the header at `9fbf7ef8e` (Draft) and at `33ea4ca66`, `58ccd3047` and `master` (Final) for the supersession | A |
| E-2 | Transaction types 85 Convert, 86 MergeInbox, 87 ConvertBack, 88 Send, 89 Clawback | `transactions.macro` @ 3.3.0, matches spec | A |
| E-3 | The Send `ZKProof` is a fixed-length bundle of a compact sigma proof plus an aggregated Bulletproof, enforced at preflight | `Protocol.h:466–471`; `ConfidentialMPTSend.cpp` length check | A |
| E-3a | Those components are 192 B and 754 B, totalling 946 B | **GAP-6 closed 2026-08-16.** `secp256k1_mpt.h:313` (192), `mpt_protocol.h:35` (754) at commit `48a3cfe` = `0.4.0-rc4`; identical at `1.0.4`. Independently **executed**: `test_bulletproof_agg` prints 754 B for m=2, 688 B for m=1. **Confirmed invariant in the participant count 2026-08-17: the `ZKProof` is 946 B at n=3 and 946 B at n=4**, as the structure implies — the sigma proof is six scalars regardless of how many recipients share the ciphertext randomness | **A** |
| E-4 | Total Send payload is **1210 B without an auditor and 1276 B with one — both now MEASURED.** The conflicting 1177 B is **wrong for the wire** | **RESOLVED 2026-08-17 by measurement.** Three `ConfidentialMPTSend` transactions serialised and accepted on standard Devnet. Each encrypted-amount field carries a **full 66-byte ElGamal pair**, not a bare 33-byte `C₂`: `SenderEncryptedAmount` 66, `DestinationEncryptedAmount` 66, `IssuerEncryptedAmount` 66, `AmountCommitment` 33, `BalanceCommitment` 33, `ZKProof` 946 = **1210 B**. The whole signed blob is **1423 B**. **Both documents were partly right and the reason matters (→ F-13):** `C₁` *is* shared, and this is **enforced by the protocol, not chosen by our harness** — a distinction REVIEW-004b HIGH-03 was right to demand and which the original evidence could not support, since it amounted to the harness observing its own input. Settled by negative control (REVIEW-004c §3): a Send built with independent per-participant randomness is refused at three layers — `mpt_get_confidential_send_proof` returns `-1` and generates nothing, `mpt_verify_send_proof` returns FAIL, and **rippled returns `tecBAD_PROOF`** where the shared-`C₁` control returns `tesSUCCESS`. The ledger nonetheless transmits `C₁` **once per participant**. The paper's 1177 is the size this would be if `C₁` were sent once, which rippled 3.3.0 does not do. The 99 B is exactly the 3 redundant 33-byte `C₁` copies at n=4. Our arithmetic was right; the paper's assumption about the wire was not. **The auditor case was measured on 2026-08-17**, closing the n=3 limitation the claims-check gate had flagged: an issuance carrying `sfAuditorEncryptionKey` was built and a four-participant Send accepted. Payload **exactly 1276 B** — `SenderEncryptedAmount` 66, `DestinationEncryptedAmount` 66, `IssuerEncryptedAmount` 66, **`AuditorEncryptedAmount` 66**, `AmountCommitment` 33, `BalanceCommitment` 33, `ZKProof` 946. Signed blob **1492 B** (n=3: 1423). The arithmetic prediction was confirmed rather than merely assumed, and **the paper's 1177 for n=4 is now refuted against a direct n=4 measurement rather than against a hypothetical** | **A on both figures, measured** |
| E-5 | **A confidential transaction costs exactly 10× the network's standard base fee.** The widely-quoted "100 drops" is that ratio against a `base_fee` of 10 and is **network-dependent, not absolute** | **Mechanism executed 2026-08-17 (REVIEW-004c §5), closing the "arithmetic not verified" gap that had stood since 2026-08-13.** Measured by ladder on Devnet where `fee` RPC reports `base_fee = 1`: `ConfidentialMPTSend` returns `telINSUF_FEE_P` at 1, 5 and 9 drops and `tesSUCCESS` at **10**, on both zero-amount and non-zero arms; a standard `AccountSet` from the same account is accepted at **1 drop**. Ratio 10:1, executed. `kConfidentialFeeMultiplier = 9` (`Protocol.h:492`); `Transactor::calculateBaseFee` still unread, but the result no longer depends on reading it. **Publish the ratio, never the absolute number** | **A on the ratio, executed; the "100 drops" figure is B and network-dependent** |
| E-6 | `kTxMaxSizeBytes = megabytes(1)` — size is not a binding constraint | `Protocol.h`, executed | A |
| E-7 | Confidential MPTs are incompatible with a non-zero `TransferFee` | Spec §6.4; enforced at `MPTokenIssuanceSet.cpp:214` | A |
| E-8 | Confidential MPTs cannot be used with the DEX, escrow or checks | Spec §A.4; RippleX docs | A |
| E-9 | Holder, issuer and auditor encryption keys cannot be rotated once registered | `tecDUPLICATE` / `tecNO_PERMISSION` paths; docs concur | A |
| E-10 | A holder who loses their ElGamal key permanently loses access | Spec §A.6; docs | A |
| E-11 | Only one auditor is supported | Spec §A.12 | A |
| E-12 | Clawback burns the entire balance, decrementing both OA and COA, crediting no account | `ConfidentialMPTClawback.cpp:170–179` | A |
| E-13 | ~~`xrpl.js@5.0.0` and `ripple-binary-codec@2.8.0` contain no Confidential MPT types or fields~~ **SUPERSEDED 2026-08-16 → E-30.** True of the versions named; **`ripple-binary-codec@2.9.0`, published 2026-08-13, ships all five types and every confidential field** | Direct inspection, 2026-08-13 (2.8.0) and 2026-08-16 (2.9.0) | A |
| E-14 | mpt-crypto is C/C++; `0.4.0-rc4` in rippled 3.3.0. ~~no bindings, no WASM target, no published package~~ **PARTLY SUPERSEDED 2026-08-16 → E-31.** A WASM target exists in CI and exports proof generation for all four proving transactions. **No published package** — `@xrplf/mpt-crypto` returns `Not found` on the npm registry as at 2026-08-16 | Repo inspection; rippled changelog PR #7813; `.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh` at `1.0.4`; npm registry query | A |
| E-15 | `MPTokensV2` (XLS-82 MPT DEX) is `Supported::No` in 3.3.0 | `features.macro` | A |
| E-16 | `SmartEscrow` (XLS-100) is absent from `features.macro` in 3.3.0 | `features.macro` | A |
| E-17 | **When set**, `sfDomainID` on the `MPTokenIssuance` gates confidential transfers, checked by `requireAuth` on both sender and destination | `ConfidentialMPTSend.cpp` preclaim; `MPTokenHelpers.cpp` | A |
| E-18 | An `MPToken` cannot be deleted once confidential fields are initialised, even at zero balance. ⚠ **Superseded upstream on 2026-09-03.** XLS-0096 PR #604 replaced this with two conditional rules in §7.2: a holder's own deletion is blocked while the issuance's `ConfidentialOutstandingAmount` is non-zero, and implicit deletion while any confidential field is present. Read at `58ccd30` on 2026-10-18 (E-316, F-29) | Spec §7.4, at the time; §7.2 at `58ccd30` for the supersession | A |
| E-19 | Writing `sfDomainID` requires **both** `PermissionedDomains` and `SingleAssetVault` enabled | `MPTokenIssuanceCreate.cpp:31–36` | A |
| E-20 | The issuer can unilaterally clear `sfDomainID`, removing the domain gate without holder consent | `MPTokenIssuanceSet.cpp:369–376` | A |
| E-21 | Clawback requires `lsfMPTCanClawback` on the issuance; without it, `tecNO_PERMISSION` | Spec §11.3.2 | A |
| E-23 | ~~ElGamal decryption bounded at 1,000,000; above that permanently undecryptable through the API~~ **CORRECTED 2026-08-16 — see F-7.** Decryption brute-forces the discrete log over a **caller-supplied window** `[range_low, range_high]`. 1,000,000 is a *documented default*, not a bound. Cost is linear in window **width**, ~2.49 µs/iteration, with **no early exit** — paid in full on every call regardless of plaintext | Read + executed at `0.4.0-rc4` (`48a3cfe`): `src/elgamal.c:128,193–247,265–366`, `include/secp256k1_mpt.h:50–88`, `src/utility/mpt_utility.cpp:511–536`. Measured: `benchmarks-2026-08-16.md` §4 | **A** |
| E-22 | (a) A `ConfidentialMPTSend` of zero is **valid**. (b) It is indistinguishable from a non-zero send — **survived THREE falsification checks under a controlled rerun, NOT proven** | **Tested 2026-08-17, then RE-TESTED under control the same day after REVIEW-004 (see REVIEW-004c §2).** (a) is **Grade A, executed**: zero-amount Sends return `tesSUCCESS` and validate. (b) remains unprovable by this method; what can be said is that three attempts to break it failed. ⚠ **"Four checks" was wrong and is retired: the old check 1 was validity, which IS limb (a), so the count used the validity claim as evidence for the indistinguishability claim.** Both review legs missed this; the Gemini leg found it. Limb (b) rests on three checks, not four. **[1] Randomisation**, the sharpest test: two zero Sends from the same account at the same balance differ in **all six** observables — `SenderEncryptedAmount`, `DestinationEncryptedAmount`, `IssuerEncryptedAmount`, `AmountCommitment`, `BalanceCommitment`, `ZKProof`. Not canonical. **[2] F-1 distinguisher** — canonical `EncZero` recomputed independently for both sender and destination matches **no** zero-Send ciphertext. **[3] Side-by-side vs non-zero Sends, CONTROLLED.** The first run was confounded — both legs caught it — comparing a 100-unit send proved at balance 250 against zero sends proved at 150, with n=1 on the control and the fee dimension argued rather than measured. Re-run with zero sends **first**, since a zero send does not move the balance: **all four arms proved at balance 80**, n=2 per arm. Result: signed blob **1423 B all four**, ZKProof **946 B all four**, `AffectedNodes` **3 all four**, identical fields mutated on identical node types (`ConfidentialBalanceInbox`+`IssuerEncryptedBalance` on the destination; `ConfidentialBalanceSpending`+`ConfidentialBalanceVersion`+`IssuerEncryptedBalance` on the sender), version **+1 all four**. **Fee threshold measured on both arms**, not deduced: a full ladder gives `telINSUF_FEE_P` at 1 and 9 drops and `tesSUCCESS` at 10 for **both** zero and non-zero — identical. **Metadata is NOT constant and E-51 was wrong about it**, but the variation tracks *destination state*, not amount: at matched destination state the arms are byte-identical (1219/1219, then 1225/1225 once the destination has merged). **No asymmetry attributable to the amount was found.** Zero sends still mutate the sender's spending ciphertext, because subtracting `Enc(0, r)` changes `C₁` — so "nothing changed" is not observable either. **Never write "confirmed": four passing checks are not a proof of indistinguishability, and an observer with resources this session did not model may still separate them** | **(a) A, executed. (b) C — survived three checks under a controlled rerun, unfalsified, not proven** |

Compact sigma standard prove **0.214 ms**, verify **0.328 ms**. Full Send bundle ≈ 34.8 ms prove / 16.4 ms verify | Measured 2026-08-16 — `docs/task-zero/benchmarks-2026-08-16.md`, full hardware + flags + spread recorded per P-8 | A |

True when made, false within the day. **This is the third time in three sessions** that an ecosystem-gap claim expired inside 24 hours (see U-8, U-9). The register's own rule — re-check registries immediately before filing, never at research time — is now evidenced three times and should be treated as binding | **Superseded** |
| E-33 | **Generating a `ConfidentialMPTSend` proof requires the sender's ElGamal secret key `sk_A` as a witness.** `sk_A` appears in the witness `w = (m, r, b, ρ, sk_A)`; balance linkage is proved as `B₂ − b·G = sk_A·B₁` precisely because the historical randomness `r_b` is unavailable to the prover. 2-special soundness extracts `sk_A`; knowledge soundness holds in the ROM under DL. **ADR-0001's central premise is confirmed.** `ConvertBack` shares the relation; `Clawback` requires `sk_iss` instead | `cmpt-compact-sigma.pdf` §2.3, §2.4 Eq. 1, Thm 4.2 (Relations 5–6), Thm 4.4, Remark 4.5 — Dore & Cenk, RippleX Research, 2026-05-12. Read in full 2026-08-16 | A |
| E-34 | **Nonce reuse in a compact-form sigma proof leaks `sk_A`, and is undetectable from the proofs.** Compact form transmits `(e, z…)` not the commitments, so reused nonces yield proofs that verify normally while allowing an observer holding both to extract the witness. ⚠ **That mitigation line is FALSE and was falsified by two independent cryptography reviewers on 2026-08-17 (REVIEW-005a, REVIEW-005b, adjudicated in REVIEW-005c §1).** `statement` does not include `TransactionContextID` as the paper defines it, while the Fiat–Shamir challenge does — so an ordinary resubmission at a changed sequence or `CB_S_Version`, with cached ciphertexts, yields two proofs sharing first-round commitments under distinct challenges. That is Theorem 4.2's extraction condition exactly. **The formula as written causes the failure it is quoted to prevent.** The correct requirement is that the nonce preimage must refine the challenge preimage — every byte that can influence the challenge, other than the commitments, must appear injectively in the derivation. See F-17 | `cmpt-compact-sigma.pdf` §6.1, Remark 6.1. **Direct input to ADR-0001 §(e) and the Rust signer — ranks above TOB-RIPCTX-5, since it needs only a coding error, not an adversary with local measurement** | A |
| E-35 | Compact-form proofs **cannot be batch-verified** — commitments are reconstructed per-proof from each proof's own challenge, leaving no cross-proof linear structure. Verification is O(n) multi-scalar multiplications per proof (11 at n=4), with no amortisation across a ledger | `cmpt-compact-sigma.pdf` Remark 6.2. Constrains any throughput claim | A |
| E-36 | XLS-96 **deliberately rejected twisted ElGamal.** Under compact form its size advantage shrinks from 292 B to an irreducible **64 B** — structural, arising from three scalar witnesses vs five — judged insufficient against specification, implementation and audit costs | `cmpt-compact-sigma.pdf` §A, Tables 5–6, §A.5. The scheme choice was quantified, not incidental — relevant background for W-PQ | A |

481:| F-28 | ⚠ **`tapFailHard` stops a `tec` reaching the LEDGER. It does not stop the ciphertext reaching the NODE you submitted to — and under a remote ledger source that node is the one F-23 is about.** The **signer** reads the destination's `HolderEncryptionKey` and the issuance's `IssuerEncryptionKey` from an endpoint, builds a `ConfidentialMPTSend` encrypted to whatever it read, proves and signs it; the **facility** then **submits that blob to the same endpoint**, because `run-facility.ts` hands the signer its own `ledgerEndpoint`. ⚠ **Both halves of that sentence were wrong for one commit** — it said *"the facility reads"*, which is the wrong component, and *"the issuer's `HolderEncryptionKey`"*, which is a field the issuer does not have. Round two's two legs found one each. ⚠ **The endpoints being the same is a property of this driver, not of `Facility`'s constructor.** A lying source therefore receives the ciphertext encrypted under its own key **before** the flag can prevent application or relay. The flag prevents *publication*; it prevents no *disclosure to the submitter*. ⚠ **So under E-151's amendment the two halves of B-15's answer are not merely unequal in strength — Part C does not cover the residual Part A was carrying at all.** With loopback the node is the operator's own, **which narrows the question to whoever can reach that node — and the issue #1 ruling puts a hostile same-host process IN SCOPE.** ⚠ **This row said the question *does not arise* on loopback, and round two's Codex leg pointed at `facility/src/loopback.ts`'s own header to refute it**: that module says in terms that the same-host adversary *"can point this at a different endpoint, or edit the local node's answers"*. A compromised local node lies about the key and reads the submitted blob exactly as a remote one does. What is true is narrower: **against an honest, operator-controlled node the disclosure has nowhere to go**, and that is the reason the ruling called loopback the protection and the flag a seatbelt | **Read at source and reasoned, 2026-08-25; NOT executed.** Found by `REVIEW-023`'s Codex leg while checking whether the executed control established what four documents said it did. ⚠ **What was executed is narrower than those documents claimed**: `tapFailHard` changed the treatment of a `tecNO_AUTH` from a plaintext `Payment` — no substituted key, no `ConfidentialMPTSend`, no `tecBAD_PROOF`, no attacker-key ciphertext. The "all `tec`" step is `Transactor.cpp:1645-1651`'s text, not this run's. **What would settle it:** a substituted-key Send under the flag against a lying source that **does** receive the submission — and then showing whether that source can recover the amount despite `fail_hard`. ⚠ **This row first prescribed *"a source that cannot obtain the blob"*, which is a different topology and cannot exhibit the disclosure F-28 is about.** Round two's Codex leg found it: an experiment that removes the mechanism cannot promote the row from reasoned to executed. The wording was inherited from `REVIEW-023c` §5, which said the same thing | A on the reading, NOT executed (E-162) |
492:| F-29 | **XLS-0096 §7.4 states the `MPToken` deletion blocker unconditionally; rippled enforces it through two paths that disagree.** §7.4: a holder's `MPToken` *"cannot be deleted from the ledger once confidential fields have been initialized, even if"* all three balances hold the canonical encrypted zero. `MPTokenHelpers.cpp:300–307` (`removeEmptyHolding`) matches it — unconditional, on the presence of any of the **four** confidential fields. `MPTokenAuthorize.cpp:86–103` (`tfMPTUnauthorize`, the holder deleting their own holding) does **not**: it returns `tecHAS_OBLIGATIONS` only when the **issuance-level** `sfConfidentialOutstandingAmount != 0`, and then tests only **two** of the four fields. With issuance-wide confidential outstanding at 0, the holding is deletable with confidential fields present. ⚠ **Not a supply-accounting defect** — a zero issuance-wide total means every holder's confidential balance is provably zero, and the inline comment says that is the reasoning. **The consequence is for integrators: the reserve is NOT permanently locked, which is what §7.4's wording would have an onboarding flow tell a user.** ⚠ **P-10 is NOT weakened and must not be read as weakened**: XLS-96 still has no key rotation and no key recovery. Deleting and recreating a holding is not rotation, and the upstream filing says so explicitly before raising the question of whether the sequence is intended ⚠ **CORRECTED 2026-08-26, same day, by a second review pass — and the correction was posted to the issue.** The conditional behaviour is **deliberate and pinned by upstream tests**: `ConfidentialTransfer_test.cpp:3156` expects `tecHAS_OBLIGATIONS` *"even if he has encrypted zero amount"* while confidential outstanding is non-zero, and `:3164` / `:3249` expect the unauthorize to **succeed** — *"can delete mptoken if outstanding confidential balance is zero"*. The unconditional path is tested too, for vault shares (`Vault_test.cpp:5972`, `ConfidentialTransfer_test.cpp:3256`). **So the two paths do not disagree by oversight; they encode different policies and both are intended, which means the defect is §7.4's WORDING and nothing else.** The filing had ended by asking a maintainer which behaviour was intended — a question the test suite answers, and which should have been checked before filing rather than after. | **2026-08-26, source reading at rippled 3.3.0. ⚠ NOT EXECUTED** — the deletion sequence has not been run on Devnet, and the filing says so in its own disclosure. Filed upstream as [XRPL-Standards #610](https://github.com/XRPLF/XRPL-Standards/issues/610) / `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md` U-50. ⚠ **Found by re-verifying U-11 against the transactor rather than against the spec** — U-11 had been graded A on a reading of §7.4 alone for ten days. **Resolved upstream:** XLS-0096 PR #604, merged 2026-09-03, moved the rule to §7.2 and states both policies, matching the code; #610 was closed on 2026-09-10 (E-316). §7.2 read at `58ccd30` on 2026-10-18 | A, not executed |
514:| R-7 | `docs/explainer/WHAT-CONFIDENTIAL-MEANS-2026-09-02.md`, limit 1, and the README before 2026-10-19: a holder who converts in 500, receives nothing and converts back 400 shows that *"100 left by transfer"* | The other 100 is either still held or has left by confidential transfer. The arithmetic bounds the balance and does not say which. Corrected in the explainer on 2026-10-21 | The README's rewrite for publication (`c561541`, 2026-10-19) carried the corrected sentence with no row, and the explainer the README links for the long form kept the old one, public from that day. Found on 2026-10-21 while drafting the hosted site's explainer from both. The error overstated what an observer learns, not what is hidden |
515:| R-8 | `web/api/capability.ts`'s wallet row and the wallet page: *"A confidential transaction needs the ElGamal secret and the account key"*, and *"needs two secrets"* | A Send, a ConvertBack and the first Convert, which registers the key, need both; a MergeInbox or a later Convert needs only the account key (TASK ZERO §3), and the facility signs its MergeInbox with the seed alone. The wallet conclusion stands, because without the first Convert there is no position. Corrected 2026-10-22 | Found by `REVIEW-061` leg A in a diagram caption that repeated the sentence. Both copies had been on the public site since publication; earlier reads of the wallet page checked its conclusion, which holds, and not this premise |

# Upstream survey, re-run 2026-10-22 before the posts

Phase 3 of `docs/PLAN-PUBLIC-LAUNCH-2026-09-22.md` requires every filing to be
re-surveyed at source before a word of either post is drafted. The previous run
(`docs/evidence/upstream-survey-2026-10-20/README.md`) is two days old, and the
session that drafts the posts runs it again first. It found that F-9's filing
was fixed and closed the day before. `CLAIMS.md` E-353.

## Method

The five steps of the 2026-10-20 run, plus one for the fix this run found:

1. `gh api repos/XRPLF/<repo>/issues/<n>`, `.../comments --paginate` and
   `.../timeline --paginate` for all eleven filings, as `nandanito`, listing
   state, close date, every comment by another account, and every
   cross-reference. Every fetch returned a body; none failed.
2. `gh pr view` for mpt-crypto #145, #149 and #152, their reviews and comments,
   `compare/5bec3364d...<head>` for #152, and `search/issues` for everything in
   `XRPLF/mpt-crypto` updated since 2026-10-19. The releases and tags list.
3. XLS-0096 at XRPL-Standards `master` (`93389999a`, unchanged since 2026-10-19)
   compared with `58ccd3047` by `cmp`. The commit list for XLS-0099's directory.
4. The docs page at xrpl-dev-portal `master` (`dfebf359f`, 2026-10-21) in English
   and Japanese, the commit list for each copy, and both rendered pages fetched
   live from xrpl.org. `@l10n` still holds `ja` and `es-ES`, and `es-ES` has no
   copy of this page.
5. `features.macro` at rippled `develop` (`025aa7193`, 2026-10-21), at `3.4.1`
   and at `3.5.0-rc1`, a tag that did not exist on 2026-10-20.
   `tools/check-amendments.ts` at 2026-10-22T03:25:30.481Z, and `server_info`
   plus `feature` on Mainnet, Testnet and Devnet at 2026-10-22T03:30:47.442Z,
   listing every amendment whose name matches *Confidential* or *Rotation*.
6. **F-9 re-run at the merged commit.** mpt-crypto fetched at `385b3611b` into
   an empty directory, rippled's `conan/` fetched at `develop` `025aa7193`,
   Conan 2.33.0 in its own virtual environment, and `CONAN_HOME` set to an empty
   directory before the first `conan` command. rippled's `conan/init.sh` run as
   mpt-crypto's README directs, then `conan graph info . --lockfile=conan.lock`.
   The control is the same command with the lockfile from the merge's parent,
   `c60a60cc8`.

exec
/bin/zsh -lc "nl -ba docs/posts/BLOG-2026-10-22-draft.md; nl -ba docs/posts/LINKEDIN-2026-10-22-draft.md; rg -n '"'^'"\\| (E-58|E-59|E-146|E-211|E-290|E-316|E-341|E-353|F-1|F-23|F-24|R-[1-8]|B-30|B-31|P-)' CLAIMS.md" in $USER_TMP/T/r062a.p1gA5h/repo
 succeeded in 0ms:
     1	# The shape of the book, not its size: what confidential transfers on the XRP Ledger hide
     2	
     3	*Status, before anything else: this is a research project on the XRP Ledger's
     4	Devnet test network. It is not audited, not production-ready, and not safe to put
     5	value behind. Its signer refuses every network but Devnet.*
     6	
     7	Can a token move confidentially on the XRP Ledger? Yes, under a standard called
     8	XLS-96, and today only on Devnet. Read on 2026-10-22 at 03:25 UTC, its amendment,
     9	`ConfidentialTransfer`, was enabled on Devnet, and supported but not enabled on
    10	Mainnet and Testnet. That reading goes stale. The repository has a script that
    11	takes a fresh one.
    12	
    13	What XLS-96 hides is narrower than the word *confidential* suggests. It hides
    14	balances, and the amounts of transfers between confidential balances. It does not
    15	hide who pays whom, when, or how often. It does not hide amounts entering or
    16	leaving confidential form. And it hides nothing from the token's issuer.
    17	
    18	Since mid-August I have been building an application on it to find out exactly
    19	where that line falls, by running transactions rather than by reading the
    20	specification. This post is what I found, what the reviews of my own work caught,
    21	and what went back upstream. The project is called W-CONF. The code and its claims
    22	register are public at
    23	[github.com/nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions](https://github.com/nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions),
    24	and a read-only demo is at
    25	[xrpl-confidential-positions.vercel.app](https://xrpl-confidential-positions.vercel.app).
    26	
    27	Ids such as *E-58* in this post are rows in the project's claims register,
    28	[`CLAIMS.md`](https://github.com/nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions/blob/main/CLAIMS.md).
    29	Each row says what supports it. Where this post and a row disagree, the row wins.
    30	
    31	I built it with Claude Code as the coding agent. The reviews described below were
    32	run by other models (Codex and Gemini) and by separate Claude sessions, each given
    33	a different packet.
    34	
    35	* * *
    36	
    37	## What XLS-96 is, briefly
    38	
    39	The XRP Ledger is public: anyone can read every account and every transaction.
    40	Multi-Purpose Tokens (MPTs, XLS-33) are one of its token types. XLS-96,
    41	Confidential Transfers for MPTs, lets a holder keep an MPT balance encrypted on
    42	that public ledger.
    43	
    44	Each confidential balance is an ElGamal ciphertext. A holder registers their own
    45	encryption key when they opt in. The ledger still has to stop anyone spending more
    46	than they hold, so every transaction that spends from a confidential balance
    47	carries zero-knowledge proofs, which validators check without learning the
    48	amount.
    49	
    50	A position moves through four transactions:
    51	
    52	- **Convert** moves tokens from a public balance into confidential form. The
    53	  amount is published.
    54	- **Send** moves an amount from one confidential balance to another. The amount is
    55	  hidden. The sender and receiver are not.
    56	- **MergeInbox** moves what a holder has received into the balance they can spend.
    57	- **ConvertBack** moves tokens back to a public balance. The amount is published.
    58	
    59	Only Send hides an amount.
    60	
    61	The specification, XLS-0096, is the work of Murat Cenk, Aanchal Malhotra, Ayo
    62	Akinyele, Peter Chen, Shawn Xie and Yinyi Qian, and has been `Final` since
    63	2026-09-03 (E-1). The compact sigma protocol behind its send proof is the work of
    64	Tess Dore and Murat Cenk at RippleX Research. The reference cryptography is
    65	[`XRPLF/mpt-crypto`](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto). This project builds on
    66	their work and claims none of it.
    67	
    68	* * *
    69	
    70	## What it hides, and what it does not
    71	
    72	**Hidden from the public:** each holder's balance, and the amount of each Send.
    73	
    74	**Public, always:**
    75	
    76	- The sender and receiver of every transfer, and when it happened, so how often
    77	  anyone transacts (P-4, P-11).
    78	- Every amount converted in or out, each one separately.
    79	- The token's total supply, and the total held in confidential form.
    80	- That a balance is exactly zero, in three cases, below.
    81	
    82	**Who can read every balance:** the issuer, always. XLS-96 keeps a second copy of
    83	each confidential balance, encrypted to the issuer's key, and no setting turns it
    84	off (P-9). An auditor can read them too, if the token names one, and only one is
    85	supported (E-11). I ran this rather than inferring it. The holder, the issuer and
    86	the auditor each decrypted the same balance, 500, and each read 475 after a
    87	25-unit Send (E-58).
    88	
    89	**Who can spend:** only whoever holds the holder's encryption secret, because a
    90	Send's proof cannot be made without it (E-33). Where that secret is derived from
    91	the account's master seed, as this project derives it, whoever holds the seed
    92	holds the secret too. The issuer cannot spend a holder's balance. If the token
    93	allows clawback, the issuer can destroy it, crediting no one, and this project's
    94	tokens allow clawback (E-12). Unless the token's flags were made unchangeable when
    95	it was created, the issuer can also turn on locking later and freeze balances.
    96	That second power I read in rippled's source and did not run (E-290).
    97	
    98	**Zero is sometimes visible.** XLS-96 writes a fixed encryption of zero, which
    99	anyone can recompute from public inputs, in three places: a new confidential
   100	balance, an inbox that a MergeInbox has just emptied, and a position clawed back.
   101	So enrolment is publicly visible. The English documentation said this value could
   102	not be told apart from other ciphertexts. I recomputed it from public inputs and
   103	matched it byte for byte against live Devnet state (F-1, E-59), reported it, and
   104	the English page was corrected in September. A balance spent down to zero holds a
   105	randomised encryption of zero, and is not recognisable (E-211).
   106	
   107	**The public numbers bound the hidden ones.** If a holder converts in 500, receives
   108	nothing, and converts back 400, anyone can work out that the other 100 is either
   109	still held or has left by confidential Send. No key is needed. I first wrote that
   110	the 100 *"left by transfer"*. That overstated what an observer learns, and it is
   111	recorded as a correction (R-7).
   112	
   113	* * *
   114	
   115	## No rotation, no recovery
   116	
   117	A registered key can never be changed (E-9). XLS-96 has no key rotation and no key
   118	recovery (P-10). If a holder loses their key, and any seed it could be re-derived
   119	from, the balance cannot be spent. The only remedy is an issuer clawback, which
   120	destroys the balance.
   121	
   122	A draft standard, XLS-99, by Aanchal Malhotra and Yinyi Qian, proposes rotation for
   123	holder, issuer and auditor keys, and recovery from key loss. On 2026-10-22,
   124	rippled's development branch and its release candidate `3.5.0-rc1` declared its
   125	amendment as not supported, and Mainnet, Testnet and Devnet did not know it (E-353).
   126	So there is no rotation or recovery in XLS-96, or on Mainnet, Testnet or Devnet,
   127	today.
   128	
   129	* * *
   130	
   131	## The example: a confidential receivables facility
   132	
   133	To test the standard against something with stakes, I built the core of a
   134	receivables facility. A bank pays a large buyer's suppliers early, against their
   135	invoices. How much a supplier draws says a lot about its cash position, so these
   136	amounts are commercially sensitive.
   137	
   138	The bank issues a token. A Dedicated Account, an ordinary XRPL account, holds the
   139	float and converts it into confidential form once. Suppliers enrol, draw down by
   140	confidential Send, and repay the same way. At the end, what is left of the float is
   141	retired in tranches by ConvertBack. It ran on Devnet on 2026-08-25, in 28
   142	validated facility transactions besides setting up the token (E-146).
   143	
   144	**Anyone can see** the size of the float, because converting it publishes the
   145	amount. They can see which suppliers enrolled, who drew and repaid, on which days,
   146	and how often. And they can see the size of every retirement tranche, because
   147	each ConvertBack publishes its own amount (F-24). My own walkthrough first said a
   148	ConvertBack published only *"the aggregate change"*. The validated transactions
   149	showed each tranche in plaintext.
   150	
   151	**Hidden from the public:** each supplier's limit, the size of each drawdown and
   152	repayment, and what each supplier still owes. The bank, as issuer, sees all of it.
   153	
   154	So it hides the shape of the book, not its size. An early version of the design
   155	claimed the facility *"shows nothing about which supplier drew how much"* (R-1).
   156	Which supplier drew, and when, is public.
   157	
   158	Two limits of that run. First, it read the ledger through a public Devnet node.
   159	The design requires a node on the same machine, and a local Devnet node never
   160	finished syncing on the hardware I had, so I accepted that as a named risk (E-148,
   161	E-151). Second, one test driver played the bank and every supplier and held every
   162	seed. The run shows that these transactions are well formed and that rippled
   163	accepts them. It does not show how custody would work.
   164	
   165	* * *
   166	
   167	## Reading the specification, and running it
   168	
   169	Several of the things above I learned only by running them:
   170	
   171	- That three different keys decrypt the same balance, through a change (E-58).
   172	- That the encryption of zero matches a value anyone can recompute (F-1).
   173	- That a balance spent to zero is not recognisable. My own observer page had
   174	  labelled every ciphertext that was not the fixed zero as *"Non-zero"*, and a
   175	  holder whose balance decrypted to 0 disproved it (E-211).
   176	- That a failing transaction is not simply rejected. A `tec` result is applied to
   177	  the ledger. The submission parameter `fail_hard` asks the node to discard it
   178	  instead. I ran both on Devnet with a plain Payment that fails with `tecNO_AUTH`:
   179	  the same signed bytes were applied without the flag, and discarded with it
   180	  (E-149).
   181	
   182	That last one matters more for confidential transactions than for plain ones,
   183	because a `tec` keeps the transaction's fields, ciphertexts included. If a signer
   184	reads a wrong destination key from a dishonest node, the amount is published
   185	encrypted to that key for good, even though the transaction fails. That case I
   186	read in rippled's source and did not run (F-23).
   187	
   188	Running is not a substitute for reading what others have already found. Several
   189	things I first recorded as findings turned out to be issues that Trail of Bits'
   190	April 2026 review had already found and Ripple had fixed.
   191	
   192	* * *
   193	
   194	## What the reviews caught
   195	
   196	Design decisions and fixes went through rounds of adversarial review. Reviewers
   197	were given deliberately different packets, so that their agreement would mean
   198	more than one reading copied three times. The repository keeps 61 numbered review
   199	records, including the ones that found me wrong. A few of the catches:
   200	
   201	- **A secret not wiped from memory, behind a comment explaining why it could not
   202	  be.** The comment said the secret's type belonged to an outside library. It was
   203	  the project's own type, and one search of its file showed it had no wiping at
   204	  all (E-200).
   205	- **A design claim that four reviewers independently ruled unsound.** I had
   206	  written that a read-only web tier *"moves no trust boundary"*. It added no
   207	  signing authority. But it re-served, to any local user, data the project
   208	  protects with file permissions (E-230).
   209	- **A release gate that passed a Content Security Policy every browser refused.**
   210	  The gate hashed inline scripts over the file's bytes. Browsers hash the text they
   211	  parse, which differs. Only loading the pages in a real browser showed it (E-334).
   212	- **Fixes that did not fix.** In one review's second round, five of the first
   213	  round's fixes had not discharged their findings. Only the reviewer that ran each
   214	  finding's own example against the fixed code found them (E-345).
   215	
   216	The lesson I keep relearning: fixing something and describing the fix are two
   217	acts that fail separately. A reviewer who reads my summary of a finding will tick
   218	the summary.
   219	
   220	* * *
   221	
   222	## Checks that catch a false sentence
   223	
   224	The working rule is that a claim needs its evidence before it is written. The
   225	claims register holds each established claim with what supports it, and twelve
   226	standing prohibitions, P-1 to P-12, on what may not be claimed at all: that XLS-96
   227	hides identities, that holders are private from the issuer, that it supports key
   228	rotation, any benchmark without its hardware and method, and any claim that a
   229	source does *not* say something without the search that would show otherwise.
   230	
   231	Prose drifts anyway, so some of it is enforced by checks that run in CI. One fails
   232	when a retracted phrase reappears without its correction. One fails when an
   233	observation filed or graded for upstream says a source does not say something,
   234	without the search recorded beside it. One keeps the register's derived indexes in
   235	step with it.
   236	
   237	They do not catch everything. The register keeps eight corrections, R-1 to R-8,
   238	each a sentence this project published and later found false. The last one, R-8,
   239	said every confidential transaction needs both of a holder's keys; a MergeInbox
   240	needs only the account key. It was on the public demo until a reviewer found it
   241	repeated in a diagram caption. And on 2026-10-18 the register still listed as open four
   242	filings that upstream had fixed in September (E-316). Nothing re-checked them until
   243	I surveyed every filing at source. That survey is now a gate on anything I publish
   244	about them, including this post.
   245	
   246	* * *
   247	
   248	## What went upstream
   249	
   250	Since 2026-08-26 this project has filed eleven issues in four XRPLF repositories.
   251	Their state on 2026-10-22, read at source (E-353):
   252	
   253	- **Four resolved in September.** Three issues against the XLS-0096 text were
   254	  resolved in the merged standard. The documentation's sentence calling the zero
   255	  value indistinguishable was corrected on the English page. The Japanese
   256	  translation was made from the text before the fix, and still carries it.
   257	- **The mpt-crypto build.** Its lockfile pinned an OpenSSL revision that the
   258	  documented package remote did not serve. Fixed on mpt-crypto's main branch on
   259	  2026-10-21, not yet in a release. I re-ran the check against the merged commit,
   260	  and it resolves.
   261	- **The fixed zero, from JavaScript.** mpt-crypto's WASM and npm packages did not
   262	  expose the function that computes the fixed encryption of zero, which is the
   263	  check an outside observer most needs. A maintainer agreed and a pull request is
   264	  approved and not merged. I have offered to write the JavaScript wrapper once it
   265	  is released.
   266	- **Three filed on 2026-10-18, against mpt-crypto, with no reply yet:** an
   267	  ambiguity in what the compact sigma paper's nonce input covers; a WASM build
   268	  script that fails outside its pinned toolchain; and a proof-size total in the
   269	  paper that assumes a ciphertext component is sent once, where rippled and
   270	  XLS-0096 send it per recipient.
   271	- **A question to XRPL-Standards**, whether a confidential balance is meant to stay
   272	  invisible to vault accounting, has had no reply in 57 days.
   273	- **A filing to rippled that listed only things done well** was closed without
   274	  comment the day it was filed. It fit neither of that repository's issue
   275	  templates. The project's filing rules now forbid a positives-only filing to a bug
   276	  tracker.
   277	
   278	I do not call any of these new, or unreported. Three reviews of this code exist.
   279	Trail of Bits' April 2026 report is the one I could read and check my findings
   280	against; a later revision of it could not be obtained. The Sherlock XRP Ledger
   281	Attackathon was a contest that covered Confidential Transfers, and its findings are
   282	not public. A Zellic scan of mpt-crypto is known to me only through three
   283	identifiers an upstream issue cites. Of the three, only Trail of Bits' April
   284	report could be checked.
   285	
   286	* * *
   287	
   288	## What it does not show
   289	
   290	- **No run against a local node.** Every Devnet read went to the public cluster
   291	  (E-151).
   292	- **Custody is accepted, not solved.** The issuer can erase or freeze the
   293	  facility's float. The Dedicated Account's key both moves the float and decrypts
   294	  every amount sent to or from it, so those two powers cannot be held apart
   295	  (E-266).
   296	- **The key derivation is unpublished.** It is specified in this repository only,
   297	  so a holder with their master seed but not this repository cannot reconstruct
   298	  their key. Treat it as lock-in until it is published.
   299	- **A hostile process running as the same user** can read the encryption secret
   300	  out of memory.
   301	
   302	* * *
   303	
   304	## Try it
   305	
   306	The demo is static pages built from one snapshot of Devnet. It signs nothing and
   307	holds no keys. It shows what an observer reads of four test accounts, including
   308	which balances are visibly zero; which encrypted copies the issuer can read,
   309	without publishing the figures; and what each kind of key can do. It does not show the facility, whose record is in the repository.
   310	
   311	To transact, you run the signer yourself, on your own machine. The README explains
   312	how to build it and run the local version of the site. It does not yet walk
   313	through creating a holder profile and funded Devnet accounts, which a transaction
   314	needs.
   315	
   316	The repository has the Rust signer, the TypeScript facility, the read-only web
   317	viewer, the claims register and the review records, under Apache-2.0.
   318	
   319	If something in this is wrong, please tell me, by an issue on the repository. The
   320	register has a section for corrections, and it has eight entries.
   321	
   322	* * *
